A number of these funding instruments and diagnostics, especially for the public schools, are already in the test, which will be used throughout the next school year. The same applies to secondary education: We also need diagnostics, and we are working on it so that the educators can test at any time where their students are. That's where I expect a lot. Of course, we also work with the educators in education and training, in order to re-train precisely and further strengthen the educators in this field. This report has always been very professional and, as has already been said, a comprehensive overview has failed. I should like to focus a little on the energy sector as an energy speaker in my contribution to the debate, and I am pleased to report that. Gross domestic consumption and final energy consumption have fallen in Austria since 2010. It is also positive that the absolute and relative contributions of renewable energy sources to national consumption have increased. The energy end-use in 2014 was 1 063 petajoule, and thus we are more than just above the target value of 1 050 Petajoule, which we set for 2020. In Austria, energy consumption accounts for about 35 percent of transport, 22 percent for private households, about 30 percent for the manufacturing sector, 11 percent for services and 2% for agriculture. A particularly positive point in the environmental control report – and I am pleased that the share of renewable energy sources increased from 23 percent to 33 percent in the years 2005 to 2014. This, ladies and gentlemen, is very encouraging, and we will also – we can say it now – achieve that target of 34 percent in 2020, which we have set ourselves. However, in order not only to achieve this goal, but perhaps to go beyond that, we need framework conditions that we have to set as legislators. In this regard, I would like to refer to the work programme “For Austria”, presented on Monday by the head of the Federal Government and presented here in Parliament yesterday and discussed in detail. In this there is a separate area, Chapter 3, which deals intensively with the topics of energy and sustainability. It is stated that in 2017 a small eco-strom novel is to be introduced and, subsequently, after the successful completion of this, a large eco-strom novel. With regard to small ecostromnovelle, it is planned that we should negotiate these over the next few weeks, and I am pleased that we will not go into the review of this law even 24 hours after the announcement here in Parliament and after the declaration by the Federal Chancellor and the Deputy Chancellor responsible for energy agents. I'll invite you, my dear. Ladies and gentlemen of the opposition – you know, this is a two-thirds matter – to agree cordially so that, as in the past with the eco-stromnovelle, there would have been almost unanimity on the last occasion, even with this law as unanimous as possible. This is an ambitious goal, but I think we might be able to achieve it in the service of the matter. Finally, because the time is already advanced, I would like to mention that there is also a positive report from the industry – I am industrial speakers too. They all know that in Austria, an above-average share of value added is provided by industry. As the figures show, there has been a complete decoupling of production and energy consumption. In 2010 an energy requirement of 661 Petajoule was determined, which in 2014 fell to 633 Petajoule. So you see, the economy, the industry is willing to make its contribution to this energy transition. Together with other sectors, we want to ensure that this positive history, which we have written in recent years and decades, can be rewritten in the field of the environment – in other words also in the energy sector. We want to make our contribution here from the ÖVP. And you are cordially invited to participate in the Ecostrom Act. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-AT_2017-02-01-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00162_d8e7022	Madam President. Mr. Minister! Ladies and gentlemen in the House! Dear taxpayers! And this time, too: Dear industrials! First of all, thank you to our Environment Ministers for this good, interesting environmental report. But when it comes to the environment, I always think about our industry automatically. Our domestic industry contributes above average to value added in the EU comparison and also employs an above average number of people, namely in Austria soon a half million people; of course, it is also necessary to add indirect jobs to this. So it is a really important factor in our country. It is most gratifying that we have so many skilled industrialists. However, I find it difficult that the industry is often labeled by society in terms of environmental protection and climate change as a bad boy – most domestic industry companies are absolutely pioneers in the field of environmental protection. The financial director of an international industrial group with headquarters in Styria recently told me that his children came home from school and said: Dad, we've heard your factory smells! – The air that leaves this factory is cleaner than the one that comes in because a clean room is operated. So I think it is already society in general, but also education policy in particular, to develop more general understanding of industry, the economy and technology and also to promote children and their interests in this direction. That we need more technicians than sociologists in our country is also a fact, but that is another debate. [[Intrusion of the Abg. Loacker]] In any case, we need a flourishing industry in our country, and the EU also sees it this way. [[Abg. Loacker: Wrong speech!]] There is now an EU strategy for reindustrialisation. This is, of course, to be implemented in an energy-efficient, low-carbon, low-emission and resource-efficient way, while maintaining value-added and employment at the same time. Some people may now say that sounds like the egg-legging wool milk sauce, but I am convinced that our domestic industry will use these specifications to develop even better and faster. [[Abg. Brunner: What defaults?]] We must not, however, go too far with our often model-student ambition, because if industry moves and produces elsewhere, we will not only lose jobs at home, but also the world climate will have nothing of it, if there are possibly less stringent regulations elsewhere, then dirt will be blown out. The environmental control report states, among other things, that it is necessary to focus largely on fossil energy in the industrialized countries by the middle of this century. In my opinion, the electricity grids are particularly important in this context. Power grids are natural monopolies, it cannot build up everyone's own power grid, so you can only transport electricity internationally via existing power grids and then need public distribution networks for local distribution. In the network, tension and frequency have to be exact, i.e. supply and demand have to be in constant balance, which naturally reveals the weaknesses of solar and wind energy – the strengths are obvious. In many international studies, Swiss scientist Silvio Borner has shown that electricity from solar and wind turbines on the grid reaches an economically absolutely critical limit of 30 percent. Therefore, if subsidies do not exceed the actual market value of green electricity by a multiple number and thus make electricity far too expensive for consumers, much remains to be done on the issue of grid development and energy storage. Decarbonisation should not mean that the customer, after paying the electricity bill, no longer has coal. So there is still much to be researched and developed, in particular I welcome any state initiative that promotes this, for example, that the research premium is now being increased to 14 percent. I also welcome any reluctance of the state in the area of bureaucracy so that our domestic industrial companies can do what they can best, namely to work, to research, to develop and to invent new products. It is known that the state has never invented anything – except new taxes; but this now has an end thanks to the government programme. In any case, it will be the pioneer spirit of our entrepreneurs, the economy, industry and its employees, who will provide us with a good service to all, especially the environment. - Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_2017-02-01-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00162_d8e8143	Next speaker: Mr Buchmayr. - Please.
ParlaMint-AT_2017-02-01-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00162_d8e8165	Mr President. Federal Minister! High house! The Environmental Control Report deals with a lot of issues from energy to sustainable development, which has already been identified. I would like to look at chemicals more closely. For me, this area is a very positive example of how the EU makes regulations for individual countries, which are often very difficult to implement, but which bring classification into the subject. As an example, the REACH Regulation ensures that only chemicals whose risk potential is sufficiently known can be traded on the European market. All input and raw materials in companies, for example, must be registered and classified. The CLP Regulation, on the other hand, ensures that all dangerous mixtures have uniform markings and must be marked with pictograms. All these regulations ultimately serve to provide us with an overview of consumers and to protect us from very dangerous chemicals. There are always grey areas and problems. An example that we have recently discussed in the Environment Committee concerns bisphenol A. This chemical is used in plastic products such as toys, sight windows and also in CD and DVD-rohlings. Bisphenol A can act like the female hormone estrogen in the human body and is responsible for the increasing infertility of men. Bisphenol A has particularly strong effects for infants too. Therefore, it has been banned at least in baby bottles since 2011. In Austria it is also banned in Schnullern and Beißringen. Another recent example is the claim by some media that the EU prohibits colorful pencils and water paints. In reality, only the limit values for lead were set down. The aim of this intensification is of course to protect children under three years of age. According to study results, a child should not absorb more than 0.5 micrograms of lead per kilogram of body weight per day. That sounds a lot. If you want a value: Each child would have to eat 18 pencils a year. Of course, it doesn't do a child. I am particularly pleased that Austria has a pioneering role in the investigation of nanomaterials. The risks and risks to people and the environment by this new technology are partly still unexplored. However, the EU-wide labelling requirement for nanomaterials in cosmetic articles, which has been valid since 2000, is constantly increasing in knowledge of their use. Since there are no nano-related detailed rules in the REACH Regulation, I would ask the Federal Government to step up its efforts to do so. Austria must continue to play a pioneering role and make serious progress on these issues! Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_2017-02-01-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00162_d8e11730	We'll get to 3. Agenda item. We are going into the debate. I call Mr Scherak. - Please.
ParlaMint-AT_2017-02-01-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00162_d8e11757	I would like to start this first reading with an anecdote that former Federal President Fischer once told me. He told me that when he came to Parliament as a young Member, a Member complained that he had not received an answer to a request to a minister. And there Alfred Maleta – I don't know if he was a minister or a national council president at the time – said: Well, read the article 52 of the Federal Constitution. It says that a Member may make a request. But it is nowhere in that the Minister also has to answer. Now it is true that the Rules of Procedure of the National Council already provide that a minister has to reply, but as a Member of Parliament we feel somehow reminded of these anecdotes, because we very often do not get the answers that actually come to us. The Ministry of Defence is particularly striking in this context – no matter which Minister. In one case, I had asked the Minister of Defence Klug. I asked him to list all the services from his department which he had awarded and to list the corresponding breakdown by order volume, contractor, type of contract and the exact content of the contract. He then told me who he's done some services with and then he writes, for example: The order volume for this was between €600 and €60 000. – Of course, that was not what I asked about. The problem is that I hardly have an option to get the answer I want. This has two reasons: On the one hand, it is a constitutional problem, because when the Constitutional Legislator amended the Constitution in 1929 and since the Federal Government is no longer elected by the National Council, the principle that an institution elected by a representative body is no longer bound to the sovereignty of the Constitution is no longer valid in connection with the Federal Government and the National Council, since the government is not elected by the National Council. Now, in principle, the silence of office is a matter of scurriles, one must say, and no longer in time, but it is even more bizarre that a countryman is very well obliged to speak about a country day and is not bound by the silence of office, but the Federal Government is still bound to the National Council. And there is a second problem that is practical: Ministers often simply don't want to answer. At present, in such a case, we Members have the only opportunity to have a short debate here to answer a question that is not sufficiently answered. Then we also have the option of not taking note of the reply here in Parliament. However, this is only possible with a majority decision, and even if that happens, it has no effect either. Finally, this only means that we have not taken note of it. I do not have the opportunity to present myself as a Member of Parliament and to say that a reply to a question has not been answered in accordance with the constitutional principle. That is why Mr Brosz and I have proposed the introduction of an organ dispute. That is, if as a Member of Parliament I feel that my question was not answered as it should be answered, then I have the opportunity to go to the Constitutional Court. I'm sure a few of my follow-up speakers will say: You can't do that, that's impossible. I'd say it's easy to do. You only have to look at the German Bundestag. There is such an organ dispute. There, by the way, every single Member has the right to stand and say: The question has not been answered the way it is planned. He may go to the Federal Constitutional Court to request and object. Such procedures are repeated, and the Members are also given the right to do so. Mr Brosz and I thought: Perhaps we do not give this right to each individual Member. We have proposed five Members for the first time. It can be discussed without further delay. However, the fact is that we need a minority right and it cannot be that we, as Members of Parliament, continue to ask questions and get back such answers, as is unfortunately still the case. There are many ministries that make it very good, some that do not make it so good, and that makes themselves particularly evident, such as the Ministry of Defence, whose answers are not sufficient. We have to change this situation, otherwise our right to interpolation will ultimately be rather ad absurd, because we can ask, but free to ask for Alfred Maleta: The Minister doesn't have to answer.

So I think that this form is a paralysis of the Constitutional Court. We have 11 480 written requests, without oral questions. If we were only to introduce such organ dispute proceedings in the case of a fraction and to withdraw and examine all files from the bodies in order to clarify the subject matter, whether that is too much or too little of an answer, then we would have the organ dispute procedure ad absurdum. ParlaMint-AT_2017-02-01-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00162_d8e12017	Mr President. Perhaps I can make some point in this regard. Parliament's right to interact – the right of Members to ask questions to the Federal Government – implies for me, apart from Maleta, the right of course to get a proper answer – but you meant it sarcastically, Mr Scherak. I think it's going deeper. It is also about the foundations of our constitution. It concerns the principle of legality and the division of powers, the interplay of executive, judicial and legislative, i.e. in the case of the interaction of the legislative body of Parliament on the one hand and the highest body of the executive, the Federal Government, on the other, and the disputes or disagreements between these two areas with the assistance of the judicial court, namely the Constitutional Court, as arbitrators. And of course it is clear that this is not particularly benevolent and not supported by the government representatives. We're going to have The Liberal Party considers this proposal or proposal to be very useful. In my opinion, this has also proved successful in Germany; but we do not need to look at Germany at all, it has also proved successful during the investigation committees. You remember: There has been blackened, blackened and blacked – up to a knowledge of the Constitutional Court, which has found that it does not go on and that the blacks should be stopped. And we all know from experience that questions are not always answered satisfactorily, very often only with: No, no, no, no, no, no, no, Yes, with: Don't touch, or: We're not responsible. My favorite formulation is: This cannot be answered for administrative reasons. So if we really take parliamentarianism and Parliament's role, among other things, as a control body of the Federal Government in the interaction of these three powers implemented in the State, we must extend, strengthen or at least use the third power in the Republic, namely the Constitutional Court, as well as the parliamentary inter-pollation law, which is the law of the German Government, to ask questions of the federal government. It is then quite able to determine to what extent the answer is sufficient or not. ParlaMint-AT_2017-02-01-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00162_d8e13087	Mr President. High house! Ladies and gentlemen! Today, in the representative of the Federal Chancellor, I can answer the urgent question. Austria is one of the winners of international trade. In the meantime, every second job depends on export. At the same time, we know that globalisation is creating increasingly winners, but also losers and losers. Many people have the feeling of losing the connection, so it is our task to ensure that profits are more justly distributed and the growth of prosperity actually arrives with the citizens. Against this background, our commitment to CETA can be understood. At the beginning, our demands were still taken as an Austrian claim. Finally, we have achieved important improvements. Both decisions were correct, namely to fight and to achieve changes, but also finally to agree with the one achieved. That was not our wish programme, but it was also at stake with the reputation of Austria as a location and European partners. [[Congratulations with the SPÖ.]] With the legally binding Supplementary Declaration on CETA, we have achieved that the European Union and its Member States can continue to regulate comprehensively. We have also ensured the protection of public services. This means that health, social and educational sectors, water and housing remain in public hands and we continue to have the right to undo privatisations. In the case of particularly controversial investment protection, we have also ensured that foreign investors cannot be improved than domestic and therefore Austrian laws cannot be removed. [[Congratulations with the SPÖ and representatives of the ÖVP.]] The legally binding declaration contains for the first time the clear commitment of the European Union to support the establishment of a multilateral commercial court as a full international commercial court. The most important thing, however, is that CETA is presented to the national parliaments for decision at all. This means that until final ratification by the national parliaments, the investment courts cannot yet be established and thus such decisions are presented to ordinary Austrian courts. My Dear Ones Ladies and gentlemen! What are the lessons of it? – The first lesson concerns TTIP. What is available in the TTIP negotiations remains far behind the agreement with Canada. Key critical areas are investment protection, regulatory cooperation, geographical designations of origin or culture. The Chancellor therefore made it clear to the European Council that there will be no consent from Austria on the basis of the existing negotiating mandate. The following principles must also be met: First: Both the provision and the way in which public services are provided shall not be affected by trade agreements. Secondly, Investment courts shall comply with the following principles: Transparency, independence, no possibility of prohibitive compensation to avoid a chilling effect, no improvement of foreign investors vis-à-vis domestic investors. Thirdly, The precautionary principle must not be affected. Fourth: Regulatory cooperation may take place exclusively voluntarily and without obligation to be implemented by the European Union and the Member States of the EU. These principles are also to be read in the Declaration made by the Chancellor of the Federal Republic of Germany to the European Council. Another important teaching concerns the way these agreements come about. Negotiations must be made more transparent in the future and all stakeholders involved must be involved. It should not be accepted that during the negotiations no information on the negotiating position will be disclosed and that it will be argued that it is now too late for changes. [[Congratulations with the SPÖ and representatives of the ÖVP.]] Trade agreements of new types cannot be penetrated with the archival method of transparency. This is all the more urgent as trade policy has increasingly become a field of political and social debate which affects many policy areas. Therefore, a much wider discussion process will also be needed in the future, in which all advantages and disadvantages can be weighed down. Only in this way can acceptance be gained in the individual Member States and the support of citizens. This requires much greater involvement of national parliaments from the very beginning. Together with the European Parliament, only they can provide European trade agreements with the necessary democratic legitimacy. The EU is today the largest trading power in the world. Our high standards of worker rights, consumer protection, environmental and health protection are also exemplary in a global comparison. This is something we can be very proud of. This also gives us every reason to act self-confidently and play an active role in the enforcement of fair rules of the game. In the sense of a European smart power, we should focus on developing standards in international trade upwards and not down and effectively implementing the interests of our employees. [[Congratulations with the SPÖ and representatives of the ÖVP.]] Only in this way will global trade create prosperity growth for all parts of society. Only in this way can the European Union provide protection against the risks of globalisation in the eyes of citizens. Only in this way can the European institutions regain confidence. [[Congratulations with the SPÖ.]] I can now come to answer the questions. Questions 1 and 2: The conditions laid down in the joint declaration of Canada, the European Union and the Member States must, at least, be fulfilled before the adoption of a government template. This concerns in particular the Code of Conduct and the compensation system for judges of the Investment Court and progress in the work on a multilateral investment court to which the dispute resolution mechanism is to be transferred under the Investment Protection Agreement. The Federal Government and the competent Federal Ministers will contribute to this work at European level after the proposals have been submitted by the European Commission. On Question 3: No, no, no, The joint declaration makes it clear that foreign investors cannot be granted more rights than domestic ones. This condition must, of course, be fulfilled. On Question 4: The Common Instrument for Interpretation of the CETA comprehensive Economic and Trade Agreement between Canada and the European Union and its Member States provides for the need to clarify, on the one hand, key implementation issues of the Investment Court planned at CETA. In addition, it is noted that the Contracting States will be committed to the establishment of a multilateral investment court in which all arbitration bodies negotiated so far in investment protection contracts are to be included. There are already preliminary work by the European Commission on this subject, and there should be already existing bodies such as the EFTA Court of Justice. On question 5: As far as is known, this is a discussion paper prepared by Professor Maurer for MEPs of the European Parliament. I can't go into details. The media-based view that the common instrument of interpretation is not a legal document, however, is in stark contradiction with the legal opinion of the European Commission and the European Council, especially on the intention of the declaration and also on the public understanding of the Parties of CETA, which is also Canada. On question 6: Austria, together with Germany on the occasion of the Council Decision on the signing, has declared that the provisional applicability of the Agreement can be terminated by a unilateral written declaration. [[Abg. Kogler: That's not true!]] This is also in line with the judgment of the German Federal Constitutional Court. On Question 7: ==References====External links== In June 2016, an opinion addressed to the competent members of the Federal Government in accordance with Article 23e B-VG concerning CETA. CETA has, among other things, been qualified as a mixed agreement by the European Union for the signing of the Austrian government representatives. A joint interpretative statement was made to CETA at the time of the operation of the Austrian government representatives, which takes into account the concerns of the federal states regarding high environmental standards, regulatory cooperation and the precautionary principle. At the time of the operation of the Federal Government, it was also achieved that the provisional application of the Union part of CETA does not cover investment protection, including the possibility of arbitration against States. [[Abg. Kogler: This is in national competence anyway!]] In a unilateral declaration by the Republic of Austria for the Protocol on the European Council meeting on 20 and 21. October 2016 highlighted the importance of the precautionary principle and the voluntary nature of regulatory cooperation. On Question 8: Having regard to the written declaration by the Federal Chancellor on the minutes of the meeting of the European Council of 20 and 21. I have already referred to October 2016. No negotiations are currently taking place with the US, and from today's point of view, it seems extremely unlikely that negotiations can be resumed on the basis of the existing mandate. The Federal Government is in principle committed to trade agreements and also supports a trade agreement with the USA, one of Austria's most important trading partners, but this must meet the criteria mentioned above. In question 9 a: In the case of TiSA, reference should be made to the above findings of TTIP. In principle, however, it should also be noted that the attempt to impose the privatisation of public services through international agreements is being rejected by us. As stated in CETA and the joint declaration, it is up to the political bodies of a state to determine which services are provided publicly and which private services are provided. On questions 9b and 9c: The lack of transparency in the negotiations on previous trade agreements has contributed substantially to a fundamental distrust of these legal instruments. It is therefore in the interests of all to make the negotiations transparent and open. The Federal Government has expressly expressed its express support for transparent negotiations in the work programme 2017/2018 presented by German Chancellors and Vice-Chancellors here yesterday. In question 9 (d): The Austrian government representatives will ensure that TiSA, like CETA, is concluded as a mixed agreement so that its entry into force also requires ratification by all Member States. Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_2017-02-01-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00162_d8e13977	Madam President. High house! Thank you for the urgent request, it gives me, as an economic spokesperson of the Liberal Party, the opportunity, among other things, to clarify some things clearly once and for all: The Freedom Party is a supporter of Free Trade Agreements, a supporter of free trade. [[Abg. Rädler: That's against it! Against TTIP!]] In view of the structure of the Austrian economy, anything else would be a small, strongly export-dependent country of white dissatisfaction. First point. [[Congratulations at the FPÖ.]] Second point: CETA and TTIP are a mogel pack, so to speak, when they are called free trade agreements. These packages contain not only free trade agreements but also very different things that we unreservedly reject, and therefore we are also against CETA and TTIP. [[Congratulations at the FPÖ.]] In this pack maybe 20 percent are free trade. I have been present at all the debriefings that have taken place in recent months, and have listened closely. If you listen carefully, you hear some sort of thing between the lines. What's in this mogel pack, on which free trade insists, actually inside? – A small part of free trade – this is the first point – in English Market Access, so market access. This is about minimizing customs duties, which is about accessing procurement markets et cetera. So that's what we're saying: Yeah, it's okay. That's free trade, it's promoted. I do not understand, however, if, for example, Chancellor Kern now in his plan, or even now it is inside the government plan, protection duties on Chinese steel or whatever is required. President Trump also goes in this other direction. What do I want to say? – If we now conclude these agreements, then free trade is likely to be relatively small. And I also think that the hopes of European companies to be able to get into the US procurement markets is an exaggeration. I only mention the Buy American Act as a keyword. So the Americans know very well who they are buying from, namely from American companies. This will also be strengthened by Trump. So there is little to nothing left of this heading of free trade. What are the two other areas of this agreement that we reject? – The so-called regulatory issues – this is the second area of a total of three areas – i.e. regulatory cooperation. We are told that there are voting and harmonisation processes in different sectors, for example, that the American pointer is no longer yellow and the European red. One wants to unify procedures and so on and so on. But what is this really about? – In debriefing: The Americans understand regulatory issues as a form of decision-making that takes place below Parliaments, i.e. a procedure for the decision-making which takes place at Parliaments, i.e. below Parliaments. For this, there is the Regulatory Cooperation Council. There is no parliamentarian in this House, but they are senior officials, Commission representatives, lobbyists and so on. I would like to explain in detail now, but remember the Regulatory Cooperation Council. Third: the rules, i.e. the market rules. [[Intrusion of the Abg. Matznetter.]] There is the arbitration procedure – we reject it; the reasons for this have already been presented fifty times, I do not need to explain it here again – and the rules of origin inside, as far as our Parmesan and our champagne are concerned, we will only have little to do with the Americans. We therefore reject the second and third areas. Of all the free trade, only a minimal balance remains. Let me now turn to what I think is really dangerous, namely regulatory issues, regulatory cooperation, cooperation. What can be understood? – After the Anglo-American law philosophy, TTIP and CETA are regarded as so-called living agreements, i.e. living paper, as a living contract. This is unusual, according to our legal understanding, unusual. What does that mean? . It is a living international agreement that can be adapted, which, after ratification, can still grow, can be further developed. Who is responsible for this development? . Exactly regulatory cooperation, i.e. the Regulatory Cooperation Council. They then establish this and develop it further, and this completely past the parliaments, completely past the legislator. Example: You could change, for example, the maximum limits on food ingredients, whatever. So this goes beyond that and is still a little bit of a legal uncertainty. In reality, however, this is the basic philosophy of this agreement. After all I have heard here today, of course, this also enables a certain procedure for those who want to put this mogel pack on the eye to us and the Austrian people under the name of free trade, although there are things inside that have nothing to do with free trade at all that are bad that are harmful – that is, the arbitration courts and precisely this regulatory commission. So you're saying, Yeah, let's just ratify a tough TTIP. We do not write things in as sharply as we do, and we also take care to see to what resistance in the population grows. We then take it out – all quiet! – and in addition, this regulatory cooperation council will then develop this piece by piece – as said: living paper! – and this is completely beyond the parliaments. We liberals reject this in principle – you will have noticed that I have not tried the chlorinated chicken for ten minutes now – for the following reasons: It is a mogel pack, on which free trade stands and in which very different things are in place, which are not at all compatible with the basic principles of our federal constitution, namely, in Article 1, which has the right to proceed from the people.

ParlaMint-AT_2017-02-01-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00162_d8e14594	Madam President. Mrs. State Secretary! Ladies and gentlemen! The concern about possible negative effects of globalisation is to be felt strongly in the population, and it is indeed very much in motion. We are currently experiencing a major change in our previous world order, both in terms of military security and in terms of the economic basis of our social societies in Europe. Of course, it is also clear that Austria – we all know – as an export country for the protection of its economy, its social state and its workers depends on good and reliable trade relations with its European and global partners. The policy of the new President of the United States, mentioned several times here, but also the decision of the British to leave the EU's internal market, will certainly change international trade relations in principle. We must ensure that this slogan “Americana First” remains a slogan and does not result in the interests of our citizens, our companies and our employees suddenly to stand behind. We need a strong EU common economic and trade policy now, because only with a strong EU can we resist these new, frighteningly selfish and aggressive tendencies, which we hear from beyond the Atlantic to this point. So we have to be clear: The conditions of world trade, on which we, too, depend fundamentally in Austria, are being redefined. Either we are looking for like-minded allies and are trying to carry out our idea, namely the idea of fair trade targeting and building on Win-win, the idea of high standards for the environment, health and social rights, or others determine the rules – that is the second side of the medal – whether it be China, India or the USA. [[Abg. Pirklhuber: Right!]] This is certainly not the solution we want, that is certainly not in our interest. No, we want to move ahead more now and make new partnerships. As has already been said, Canada is certainly for us a partner with great common interests and comparable standards. CETA – and I say – can therefore become an important and good agreement. It is crucial that we also implement it rationally. To this end, we have expressed ourselves several times and clearly both in plenary and in the Federal Government. There are still serious weaknesses in protecting our standards in CETA, in protecting the welfare of the general public, in the arbitration courts – that was the subject for a long time now – but perhaps one can also admit to the arbitrators a development by saying there are first steps and more need to follow. Maybe that's not all that's going on in one day. Here it is easy to make changes in this sense. Nevertheless, it remains clear – and the chancellor has made this clear in Brussels as well – that: The arbitration courts are clearly excluded from the provisional application, and the approval of this Parliament will only finally be given if, where weaknesses exist, there is a clear improvement. We want good quality and transparent trade agreements. This has shown once again the new work programme of the government. But we certainly do not get these through the USA and China, as I have already said; we will only achieve this if we act ourselves. Transparent and good means that we are continuing to treat CETA and then all the other agreements here in Parliament – and above all through this popular desire, whose criticisms we will debate very seriously and very thoroughly. A self-evangelistic policy cannot be our way, that is practically impossible for a small country like Austria. However, we want trade agreements, which the Austrians can also stand behind, because they are fair because they are environmentally friendly and social, and because they are safeguarding jobs here, rather than endangering them. [[Complementary of the Abg. Kucharovich]] For this we will continue to work and negotiate in the next few weeks, here in plenary, in the Council in Brussels and with our trading partners worldwide. - Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_2017-02-01-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00162_d8e15526	(Parliament adopted the legislative resolution) Dear Mrs Duzdar, Secretary of State! Ladies and gentlemen. Dear listeners on the gallery and in front of the TV screens! - Estimated Mrs. Dr. Karl, you have put into play the mixed agreement that our Minister for Economic Affairs has requested here. Now it is almost up to the national parliaments to vote on this whole of the debate. In this context, I recall the survey on CETA and TTIP, which has made it quite clear that this mixed agreement is not intended to prevent the provisional CETA agreement. No, this mixed agreement is only for the purpose of stating that the European Commission has been instructed to negotiate this convergence, and that at the same time the rest of the legal systems that concern the national states can be voted by the national states. That is to say, in the clear text, we are talking here of a legislative matter which is determined by the EU and by the national Parliament about 15 percent. If we now look forward to this CETA agreement in happy expectation, then we have to consider what it means when it means that we are the largest economic power on this globe. That means in the clear text: This European Union is the miracle of the last history. The question for me is: Who were the true winners of accession to the European Union? That was the small- and middle class? Were these our farmers? Or was it actually those representing the large companies or the large companies or the large transport companies? I know in all cases that, as we now have group taxation, the banks have been the great beneficiaries of this European Union, because they were able to balance their losses in the former Eastern states with our taxpayers in Austria. And that must be stopped. If a large bank generates 690 million € in profits in Austria and then only 19 million € will have to pay taxes in Austria because it can save so much taxes on us due to the losses caused by group taxation, then this is the completely wrong way. We also have huge differences in gross domestic product in this common Europe. We have a long and difficult path ahead of us to bring the gross domestic product to an almost equal level, just as we should raise social standards in this European single state to the same level. When I look at what's going on in Bulgaria or Portugal and compare Germany and Austria, I have to say: We still have a lot of work in front of us. Thanks to our farmers our food is very high quality, and it is known that health awareness is increasing significantly throughout the developed world. So there's absolutely a chance. Walser.]] In any event, the full text of the Canada-EU agreement has been public and accessible for two years, while the US negotiators have regularly shrouded themselves in silence. TTIP would also have gone far beyond CETA regulation: In CETA, importers must continue to apply local standards, and best practice ideas should be exchanged through the authorities – that’s it. CETA is also quite different from TTIP in the dispute resolution, because CETA is a permanent court with permanent judges and with a possibility of appeal. Again and again, the accusation in the room is that all this is done only for the large corporations. I came from the private sector, from a company, and I thought I was questioning that personally. So I have talked to some well-known industrialists and managers of companies about free trade agreements, and they have told me that they do not really need it. After all, it's quite simple: If their product is competitive, it is asked and sold all over the world, customs duties to or from. Otherwise, it already looks like SMEs: As a matter of fact, the customs duties may be decisive whether they are exported or not. For example, an Austrian jam manufacturer who supplies to Canada would have to pay 13 percent customs and 8 percent for vinegar cucumbers, which naturally makes exports unattractive. However, worldwide trade is of course about more than customs on cucumbers. Looking at trade contracts over the last thirty years, China, India, Thailand, South Korea and Malaysia have developed enormously. Hundreds of millions of people have been liberated from poverty and especially from the yoke of Communism. Globalisation has dramatically reduced the world's inequality, but the return side is that many production companies have migrated from our highly developed and expensive countries to these cheaper countries, for example, the Portuguese textile industry has broken down. In particular, low-qualified workers are losers of globalisation – and yes, there are really too many losers of globalisation. This is precisely what we have in our developed Western countries created the social systems that need to be ridden and ridden. But this is becoming a problem with the social systems right now, where people from other countries and continents have come to believe that they too could benefit from these social systems. The social state becomes a magnet, and that is the real challenge of the future. With us, the social state is the meaningful complement of a world-wide free trade policy, but mass immigration cannot be designed. Finally, I quote Milton Friedman, Nobel Prizewinner and one of the most influential economists of the 20th century. 19th century: You can have open borders or you can have a welfare state, but you cannot have both. - Thank you.

Dear spectators, what can be understood by them? – That is, if the State A intends to amend a certain law, if it has a contract with State B, it must inform that State in advance of Parliament before parliamentary deliberations. What's that supposed to be good for? – In order for State B to influence the further law enforcement. That is to say, this agreement is not an agreement that decides on something negotiated, existing, to which one can say yes or no, but it is based on the principle that it is constantly developing and expanding. Specific example: A company in state A has discovered that in state B, because of lack of environmental legislation, it is very lucrative to enter the timber trade, has been massively rowed there without reforestation. This is, of course, a pleasant arrangement if the state is strong; if the state – and we have seen it several times – is not as strong as ours, if it likes to surrender, always lets five be straight, then of course this is a serious threat. This Treaty allows economic interests to influence the laws of lobbying interests. And that's why we won't agree.

ParlaMint-AT_2017-02-01-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00162_d8e16186	High house! My dear viewers! In human history, there have been so many errors, as we all know. The nuclear power, for example, was such a mistake. There has always been the argument that one is anti-progression, one wants to go back to the cave, one does not want people to move forward and implement something positive. The strain on the matter is that there is no thought about whether there could be a negative impact on health. What you're thinking about is the following: You want to make more sales, you want to have more income, you want to profit more, and you want to change the properties of these foods in order to be able to process them more industrially; whether there are health problems with the consumer, that's why nobody cares in the US. Now we are in the Trade Agreement. In fact, we have a creed which has protected us from these foods here in Europe so far, namely that we say: When someone places such food on the market, he must prove that she I'm sorry, but I'm not sure I'm not hurt. In the U.S. it is exactly the other way round, that is, those who are critical of this need to prove that these foods pose a threat. That is what we call the so-called precautionary principle. We have kept this hard – against all resistances, against all those who exert pressure from industry on all possible people in order to finally make the blessings of genetic engineering available in Europe. That is the background to this agreement. We've heard it already today: It's not about free trade, it's about the food sector, that all those foods that are fed there with genetic engineering, hormones, all the stupidities that come from the United States again, like nuclear power, that you want to export all this to Europe, because the industry on the ground wants it that way. Now many say: We don't have to buy it. Only: If you read the contracts exactly, you see, it is not genetically modified, but it is processed, that is, it is then in a processed food, and then it is forbidden to mark it. If we label it and say that the genetically modified food is from the USA, and no one would buy it anymore, then there would be a damage to the farms, they could complain and would still be right. That's the problem. That is, what is being done here with this agreement is nothing more than trying to subdue these low-quality food-only foods to European consumers, and if the Europeans do not swallow it, they will be sued. So you can bring it to a simple formula. That's the background. [[Congratulations on the Stronach team.]] Now many say that I am anti-progression, because genetic engineering also brings many advantages such as more yield and better properties in the sense of further processing in industry. – Yes, that’s all right, but look at where this led us! We now have foods that have nothing more to do with the ingredients. Food is becoming more and more inferior, the consumer ultimately has to bear the problems. That's why: I think a lot of free trade, I think a lot of progress, I don't have a problem with when we get cheap drilling machines from China by €20 that are already giving up the mind after the third drill hole. I have no problem with this, no one gets hurt, but if we get food on the table, and as consumers we can no longer decide whether we want to get it on the table that makes us sick in the long term – and there are enough studies to prove it – then it's a problem and then I'm not progressive, because we don't need any progress in this area. [[Congratulations on the Stronach team.]] We need it everywhere, for all other things, but we don't need it with the food, so that on the one hand, we get inferior foods on the table, which only benefit those who produce them, and on the other hand, we kill our farmers and, finally, it's not even possible for us to take care of ourselves in the crisis situation. That's the problem. That's why: This agreement must be rejected. So think about it again, inform yourself, and you'll see that's a snap idea! Thank you.

Stefan.]] I promise to all those who have been involved: We Greens will use all the possibilities we have here in Parliament to prevent this agreement. Thank you.
